It’s not all the time, exactly, but when I start with certain comfort foods, I tell myself, “Maria, that’s enough now,” and then… my hand just goes right back to the pot anyway. Like I can’t quite make myself stop. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you intended happen?\n\nPatient: Um… I’d say most weeks, I suppose. Not every day or anything like that. Since my friend passed, it’s been more noticeable—usually once, sometimes twice a week. It tends to be those quiet afternoons, after choir or after I’ve been out in the garden. Is that… the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of how much you're eating during those times when you go back for more?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I don’t feel very in control then. I mean, I’ll tell myself, Maria, that’s enough now, and I do mean it. But then my hand just goes back to the pot anyway, like I can’t quite make myself stop. It sounds silly when I say it out loud, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating more than you intended?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I don’t like admitting that. Afterward I feel ashamed, and sad too. It’s like… food used to feel like family and caring, you know? And then I’ve made it into something I don’t want anyone at church knowing about. That sounds awful, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're eating very quickly during those times when you can't seem to stop?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I’ve always been a slow eater, really, but lately when this happens, I’ll be standing there by the counter and eating quite fast. Hardly tasting it, if I’m honest… the next bite is already in my mouth before I’ve thought about it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're aware of what's happening while you're eating during those times when you can't seem to stop?\n\nPatient: I’m not quite sure how to answer that. I mean, I know I’m there, if that makes sense… it’s not like I black out or anything. But I don’t always pay much attention in the moment. I suppose I’m more focused on just getting the next bite. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, doctor. I don’t weigh myself much… I don’t like to, if I’m honest. My clothes might feel a little different some days, but I couldn’t tell you if it’s a significant change. Is that alright to say?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating an excessively large amount of food during those episodes?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose so. I don’t like to say it that way, but some afternoons, after choir or if I’ve been out working in the garden, I’ll make myself a plate of rice and beans, or maybe chicken stew… and then I keep going back for more. Before I really admit it to myself, I’ve eaten what’s probably enough for two or three meals. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing something to counteract the calories, like exercising or vomiting?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any real way. I mean, I might tell myself I’ll be more careful later, or maybe take a little walk if I feel too full, but I don’t make myself sick or anything like that. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean by “make up for it.”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Not every day or anything like that. Since my friend passed, it’s been more noticeable—usually once, sometimes twice a week. It tends to be those quiet afternoons, after choir or after I’ve been out in the garden. Is that… the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of how much you're eating during those times when you go back for more?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I don’t feel very in control then. I mean, I’ll tell myself, Maria, that’s enough now, and I do mean it. But then my hand just goes back to the pot anyway, like I can’t quite make myself stop. It sounds silly when I say it out loud, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating more than you intended?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I don’t like admitting that. Afterward I feel ashamed, and sad too. It’s like… food used to feel like family and caring, you know? And then I’ve made it into something I don’t want anyone at church knowing about. That sounds awful, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're eating very quickly during those times when you can't seem to stop?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Hardly tasting it, if I’m honest… the next bite is already in my mouth before I’ve thought about it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're aware of what's happening while you're eating during those times when you can't seem to stop?\n\nPatient: I’m not quite sure how to answer that. I mean, I know I’m there, if that makes sense… it’s not like I black out or anything. But I don’t always pay much attention in the moment. I suppose I’m more focused on just getting the next bite. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, doctor. I don’t weigh myself much… I don’t like to, if I’m honest. My clothes might feel a little different some days, but I couldn’t tell you if it’s a significant change. Is that alright to say?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating an excessively large amount of food during those episodes?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose so. I don’t like to say it that way, but some afternoons, after choir or if I’ve been out working in the garden, I’ll make myself a plate of rice and beans, or maybe chicken stew… and then I keep going back for more. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing something to counteract the calories, like exercising or vomiting?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any real way. I mean, I might tell myself I’ll be more careful later, or maybe take a little walk if I feel too full, but I don’t make myself sick or anything like that. Like after choir, or after I’ve been out in the garden, I’ll make a plate of rice and beans or chicken stew, and then I keep going back. I don’t mean to make it sound terrible, but before long I’ve had what’s really enough for two or three meals. Is that the sort of time frame you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to prevent weight gain after these episodes by not eating for a while?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I do that… at least not on purpose. I might not feel very hungry right after, or I may tell myself I’ll just have something lighter later, but I don’t set out to stop eating for a long while to keep from gaining weight. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control how much you're eating during those episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that is what it feels like, though I don’t like to say it that strongly. Once I start on those comfort foods, I’ll tell myself, “Maria, that’s enough now,” but then my hand goes right back to the pot anyway. It feels like I should be able to stop, but in that moment I just… don’t. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are times when you eat a large amount of food and feel like you've lost control of your eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but that is really what brings me in. Once I start eating those comfort foods, I’ll tell myself, “Maria, that is enough,” and then my hand still goes back to the pot, like I can’t quite make myself stop. Is that the kind of loss of control you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean by “make up for it.”\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat this extra food within a short period of time, like less than two hours?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think usually it is in a short time, probably within a couple of hours. Like after choir, or after I’ve been out in the garden, I’ll make a plate of rice and beans or chicken stew, and then I keep going back. I don’t mean to make it sound terrible, but before long I’ve had what’s really enough for two or three meals. Is that the sort of time frame you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to prevent weight gain after these episodes by not eating for a while?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I do that… at least not on purpose. I might not feel very hungry right after, or I may tell myself I’ll just have something lighter later, but I don’t set out to stop eating for a long while to keep from gaining weight. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control how much you're eating during those episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that is what it feels like, though I don’t like to say it that strongly. Once I start on those comfort foods, I’ll tell myself, “Maria, that’s enough now,” but then my hand goes right back to the pot anyway. It feels like I should be able to stop, but in that moment I just… don’t. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are times when you eat a large amount of food and feel like you've lost control of your eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but that is really what brings me in. Once I start eating those comfort foods, I’ll tell myself, “Maria, that is enough,” and then my hand still goes back to the pot, like I can’t quite make myself stop. Is that the kind of loss of control you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed or emotional right before these episodes of eating more than you intended?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve paid close enough attention to that, honestly. I mean, I suppose I have my moods like anyone, but I don’t know that I can say it’s always stress or being emotional right before. Sometimes it just sort of happens, and then I realize I’ve eaten more than I meant to. Is that alright to say?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Not every day or anything like that. Since my friend passed, it’s been more noticeable—usually once, sometimes twice a week. It tends to be those quiet afternoons, after choir or after I’ve been out in the garden. Is that… the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of how much you're eating during those times when you go back for more?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I don’t feel very in control then. I mean, I’ll tell myself, Maria, that’s enough now, and I do mean it. But then my hand just goes back to the pot anyway, like I can’t quite make myself stop. It sounds silly when I say it out loud, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating more than you intended?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I don’t like admitting that. Afterward I feel ashamed, and sad too. It’s like… food used to feel like family and caring, you know? And then I’ve made it into something I don’t want anyone at church knowing about. That sounds awful, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're eating very quickly during those times when you can't seem to stop?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I’ve always been a slow eater, really, but lately when this happens, I’ll be standing there by the counter and eating quite fast. Hardly tasting it, if I’m honest… the next bite is already in my mouth before I’ve thought about it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're aware of what's happening while you're eating during those times when you can't seem to stop?\n\nPatient: I’m not quite sure how to answer that. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m a little embarrassed to say it, but I guess I’m here because of my eating. It’s not all the time, exactly, but when I start with certain comfort foods, I tell myself, “Maria, that’s enough now,” and then… my hand just goes right back to the pot anyway. Like I can’t quite make myself stop. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you intended happen?\n\nPatient: Um… I’d say most weeks, I suppose. Not every day or anything like that. Since my friend passed, it’s been more noticeable—usually once, sometimes twice a week. It tends to be those quiet afternoons, after choir or after I’ve been out in the garden. Is that… the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of how much you're eating during those times when you go back for more?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I don’t feel very in control then. I mean, I’ll tell myself, Maria, that’s enough now, and I do mean it. But then my hand just goes back to the pot anyway, like I can’t quite make myself stop. It sounds silly when I say it out loud, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating more than you intended?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I don’t like admitting that. Afterward I feel ashamed, and sad too. It’s like… food used to feel like family and caring, you know? And then I’ve made it into something I don’t want anyone at church knowing about. That sounds awful, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're eating very quickly during those times when you can't seem to stop?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I’ve always been a slow eater, really, but lately when this happens, I’ll be standing there by the counter and eating quite fast. Hardly tasting it, if I’m honest… the next bite is already in my mouth before I’ve thought about it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're aware of what's happening while you're eating during those times when you can't seem to stop?\n\nPatient: I’m not quite sure how to answer that. I mean, I know I’m there, if that makes sense… it’s not like I black out or anything. But I don’t always pay much attention in the moment. I suppose I’m more focused on just getting the next bite. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, doctor. I don’t weigh myself much… I don’t like to, if I’m honest. My clothes might feel a little different some days, but I couldn’t tell you if it’s a significant change. Is that alright to say?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating an excessively large amount of food during those episodes?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose so. I don’t like to say it that way, but some afternoons, after choir or if I’ve been out working in the garden, I’ll make myself a plate of rice and beans, or maybe chicken stew… and then I keep going back for more. Before I really admit it to myself, I’ve eaten what’s probably enough for two or three meals. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing something to counteract the calories, like exercising or vomiting?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any real way. I mean, I might tell myself I’ll be more careful later, or maybe take a little walk if I feel too full, but I don’t make myself sick or anything like that. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean by “make up for it.”\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat this extra food within a short period of time, like less than two hours?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think usually it is in a short time, probably within a couple of hours. Like after choir, or after I’ve been out in the garden, I’ll make a plate of rice and beans or chicken stew, and then I keep going back. I don’t mean to make it sound terrible, but before long I’ve had what’s really enough for two or three meals. Is that the sort of time frame you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to prevent weight gain after these episodes by not eating for a while?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I do that… at least not on purpose. I might not feel very hungry right after, or I may tell myself I’ll just have something lighter later, but I don’t set out to stop eating for a long while to keep from gaining weight. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control how much you're eating during those episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that is what it feels like, though I don’t like to say it that strongly. Once I start on those comfort foods, I’ll tell myself, “Maria, that’s enough now,” but then my hand goes right back to the pot anyway. Not every day or anything like that. Since my friend passed, it’s been more noticeable—usually once, sometimes twice a week. It tends to be those quiet afternoons, after choir or after I’ve been out in the garden. Is that… the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of how much you're eating during those times when you go back for more?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I don’t feel very in control then. I mean, I’ll tell myself, Maria, that’s enough now, and I do mean it. But then my hand just goes back to the pot anyway, like I can’t quite make myself stop. It sounds silly when I say it out loud, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating more than you intended?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I don’t like admitting that. Afterward I feel ashamed, and sad too. It’s like… food used to feel like family and caring, you know? And then I’ve made it into something I don’t want anyone at church knowing about. That sounds awful, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're eating very quickly during those times when you can't seem to stop?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I’ve always been a slow eater, really, but lately when this happens, I’ll be standing there by the counter and eating quite fast. Hardly tasting it, if I’m honest… the next bite is already in my mouth before I’ve thought about it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're aware of what's happening while you're eating during those times when you can't seem to stop?\n\nPatient: I’m not quite sure how to answer that. I mean, I know I’m there, if that makes sense… it’s not like I black out or anything. But I don’t always pay much attention in the moment. I suppose I’m more focused on just getting the next bite. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, doctor. I don’t weigh myself much… I don’t like to, if I’m honest. My clothes might feel a little different some days, but I couldn’t tell you if it’s a significant change. Is that alright to say?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating an excessively large amount of food during those episodes?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose so. I don’t like to say it that way, but some afternoons, after choir or if I’ve been out working in the garden, I’ll make myself a plate of rice and beans, or maybe chicken stew… and then I keep going back for more. Before I really admit it to myself, I’ve eaten what’s probably enough for two or three meals. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing something to counteract the calories, like exercising or vomiting?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any real way. I mean, I might tell myself I’ll be more careful later, or maybe take a little walk if I feel too full, but I don’t make myself sick or anything like that. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean by “make up for it.”\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat this extra food within a short period of time, like less than two hours?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think usually it is in a short time, probably within a couple of hours. Like after choir, or after I’ve been out in the garden, I’ll make a plate of rice and beans or chicken stew, and then I keep going back. I don’t mean to make it sound terrible, but before long I’ve had what’s really enough for two or three meals. Is that the sort of time frame you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to prevent weight gain after these episodes by not eating for a while?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I do that… at least not on purpose. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but that is really what brings me in. It tends to be those quiet afternoons, after choir or after I’ve been out in the garden. Is that… the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of how much you're eating during those times when you go back for more?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I don’t feel very in control then. Afterward I feel ashamed, and sad too. It’s like… food used to feel like family and caring, you know? And then I’ve made it into something I don’t want anyone at church knowing about. I’ve always been a slow eater, really, but lately when this happens, I’ll be standing there by the counter and eating quite fast. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're aware of what's happening while you're eating during those times when you can't seem to stop?\n\nPatient: I’m not quite sure how to answer that. I mean, I know I’m there, if that makes sense… it’s not like I black out or anything. But I don’t always pay much attention in the moment. I suppose I’m more focused on just getting the next bite. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m a little embarrassed to say it, but I guess I’m here because of my eating. Like I can’t quite make myself stop. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you intended happen?\n\nPatient: Um… I’d say most weeks, I suppose. Not every day or anything like that. Since my friend passed, it’s been more noticeable—usually once, sometimes twice a week. It tends to be those quiet afternoons, after choir or after I’ve been out in the garden. Is that… the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of how much you're eating during those times when you go back for more?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I don’t feel very in control then. I mean, I’ll tell myself, Maria, that’s enough now, and I do mean it. But then my hand just goes back to the pot anyway, like I can’t quite make myself stop. It sounds silly when I say it out loud, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating more than you intended?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I don’t like admitting that. Afterward I feel ashamed, and sad too. It’s like… food used to feel like family and caring, you know? And then I’ve made it into something I don’t want anyone at church knowing about. That sounds awful, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're eating very quickly during those times when you can't seem to stop?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I’ve always been a slow eater, really, but lately when this happens, I’ll be standing there by the counter and eating quite fast. Hardly tasting it, if I’m honest… the next bite is already in my mouth before I’ve thought about it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're aware of what's happening while you're eating during those times when you can't seem to stop?\n\nPatient: I’m not quite sure how to answer that. I mean, I know I’m there, if that makes sense… it’s not like I black out or anything. But I don’t always pay much attention in the moment. I suppose I’m more focused on just getting the next bite. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, doctor. I don’t weigh myself much… I don’t like to, if I’m honest. My clothes might feel a little different some days, but I couldn’t tell you if it’s a significant change. Is that alright to say?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating an excessively large amount of food during those episodes?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose so. I don’t like to say it that way, but some afternoons, after choir or if I’ve been out working in the garden, I’ll make myself a plate of rice and beans, or maybe chicken stew… and then I keep going back for more. Before I really admit it to myself, I’ve eaten what’s probably enough for two or three meals. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing something to counteract the calories, like exercising or vomiting?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any real way. I mean, I might tell myself I’ll be more careful later, or maybe take a little walk if I feel too full, but I don’t make myself sick or anything like that. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean by “make up for it.”\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat this extra food within a short period of time, like less than two hours?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think usually it is in a short time, probably within a couple of hours. Like after choir, or after I’ve been out in the garden, I’ll make a plate of rice and beans or chicken stew, and then I keep going back. I don’t mean to make it sound terrible, but before long I’ve had what’s really enough for two or three meals. Is that the sort of time frame you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to prevent weight gain after these episodes by not eating for a while?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I do that… at least not on purpose. I might not feel very hungry right after, or I may tell myself I’ll just have something lighter later, but I don’t set out to stop eating for a long while to keep from gaining weight. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control how much you're eating during those episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that is what it feels like, though I don’t like to say it that strongly. Once I start on those comfort foods, I’ll tell myself, “Maria, that’s enough now,” but then my hand goes right back to the pot anyway. It feels like I should be able to stop, but in that moment I just… don’t. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are times when you eat a large amount of food and feel like you've lost control of your eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but that is really what brings me in. Once I start eating those comfort foods, I’ll tell myself, “Maria, that is enough,” and then my hand still goes back to the pot, like I can’t quite make myself stop. Is that the kind of loss of control you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed or emotional right before these episodes of eating more than you intended?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve paid close enough attention to that, honestly. I know that may sound silly, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel upset or distressed during these episodes when you can't seem to stop eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do feel upset, though I don’t always notice it right in the middle at first. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m a little embarrassed to say it, but I guess I’m here because of my eating. It’s not all the time, exactly, but when I start with certain comfort foods, I tell myself, “Maria, that’s enough now,” and then… my hand just goes right back to the pot anyway. Like I can’t quite make myself stop. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you intended happen?\n\nPatient: Um… I’d say most weeks, I suppose. Not every day or anything like that. Since my friend passed, it’s been more noticeable—usually once, sometimes twice a week. It tends to be those quiet afternoons, after choir or after I’ve been out in the garden. Is that… the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of how much you're eating during those times when you go back for more?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I don’t feel very in control then. I mean, I’ll tell myself, Maria, that’s enough now, and I do mean it. But then my hand just goes back to the pot anyway, like I can’t quite make myself stop. It sounds silly when I say it out loud, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating more than you intended?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I don’t like admitting that. Afterward I feel ashamed, and sad too. It’s like… food used to feel like family and caring, you know? And then I’ve made it into something I don’t want anyone at church knowing about. That sounds awful, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're eating very quickly during those times when you can't seem to stop?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I’ve always been a slow eater, really, but lately when this happens, I’ll be standing there by the counter and eating quite fast. Hardly tasting it, if I’m honest… the next bite is already in my mouth before I’ve thought about it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're aware of what's happening while you're eating during those times when you can't seem to stop?\n\nPatient: I’m not quite sure how to answer that. I mean, I know I’m there, if that makes sense… it’s not like I black out or anything. But I don’t always pay much attention in the moment. I suppose I’m more focused on just getting the next bite. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, doctor. I don’t weigh myself much… I don’t like to, if I’m honest. My clothes might feel a little different some days, but I couldn’t tell you if it’s a significant change. Is that alright to say?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating an excessively large amount of food during those episodes?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose so. I don’t like to say it that way, but some afternoons, after choir or if I’ve been out working in the garden, I’ll make myself a plate of rice and beans, or maybe chicken stew… and then I keep going back for more. Before I really admit it to myself, I’ve eaten what’s probably enough for two or three meals. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing something to counteract the calories, like exercising or vomiting?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any real way. I mean, I might tell myself I’ll be more careful later, or maybe take a little walk if I feel too full, but I don’t make myself sick or anything like that. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean by “make up for it.”\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat this extra food within a short period of time, like less than two hours?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think usually it is in a short time, probably within a couple of hours. Like after choir, or after I’ve been out in the garden, I’ll make a plate of rice and beans or chicken stew, and then I keep going back. I don’t mean to make it sound terrible, but before long I’ve had what’s really enough for two or three meals. Is that the sort of time frame you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to prevent weight gain after these episodes by not eating for a while?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I do that… at least not on purpose. I might not feel very hungry right after, or I may tell myself I’ll just have something lighter later, but I don’t set out to stop eating for a long while to keep from gaining weight. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control how much you're eating during those episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that is what it feels like, though I don’t like to say it that strongly. Once I start on those comfort foods, I’ll tell myself, “Maria, that’s enough now,” but then my hand goes right back to the pot anyway. It feels like I should be able to stop, but in that moment I just… don’t. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are times when you eat a large amount of food and feel like you've lost control of your eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but that is really what brings me in. Once I start eating those comfort foods, I’ll tell myself, “Maria, that is enough,” and then my hand still goes back to the pot, like I can’t quite make myself stop. Is that the kind of loss of control you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed or emotional right before these episodes of eating more than you intended?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve paid close enough attention to that, honestly. I mean, I suppose I have my moods like anyone, but I don’t know that I can say it’s always stress or being emotional right before. Sometimes it just sort of happens, and then I realize I’ve eaten more than I meant to. Is that alright to say?\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel guilty or ashamed immediately after these episodes where you feel like you've lost control of your eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, I’m afraid. I don’t like admitting that. Afterward I feel ashamed, and sad too, like I’ve taken something that used to feel like family and caring and turned it into something I have to hide from people at church. I know that may sound silly, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel upset or distressed during these episodes when you can't seem to stop eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do feel upset, though I don’t always notice it right in the middle at first. It’s more like I hear myself saying, Maria, that’s enough now, and then I still keep reaching back, like my hand goes to the pot before I can stop it. That part is distressing, yes… because I don’t feel quite in charge of myself. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is it usually specific types of food that you lose control with when eating, like the comfort foods you mentioned?\n\nPatient: Yes… it does seem to be those kinds of comfort foods more than anything. Not that I mean to make it sound dramatic, but once I start, I’ll tell myself, Maria, that’s enough now, and then my hand still goes back to the pot like I can’t quite make myself stop. Since my friend passed, that’s when it’s been coming up more. Does that sound like the kind of answer you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your loss of control while eating usually triggered by specific events or emotions?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s more the feeling that comes up than one exact event.
